                return gptText.replace(/\^|\*|#|\$|\"|\'|\=|\,|\!|\+|\~|\;|\(|\)|\<|\>|\[|\]|\?|\%|\:|\@|\;|\&|\||\{|\}|\\|/g, '');
            }
            return "blank";
        }

        const identities = window.adobeDataLayer?.[0]?.user?.identities || [];
        const segments = identities
            .filter(identity => identity.type === 'AudienceSegmentsUser')
            .map(identity => identity.name)
            .filter(name => name);

        googletag.pubads()
            .setTargeting('property', 'value-of-property')    // Change and duplicate this line for as many additional keywords to send
            .setTargeting('doi', gptClean('10.1002/adma.202400307')) // Publication DOI
            .setTargeting('journalDoi', gptClean('10.1002/(ISSN)1521-4095')) // journal DOI
            .setTargeting('journalCode', gptClean('15214095')) // journal code
            .setTargeting('pbPage', gptClean(gptPage))
            .setTargeting('pbSubPage', gptClean(gptSubPage))
            .setTargeting('AudienceSegmentsUser', segments)
            .setTargeting('urlpath', gptClean(document.location.pathname))
			.setTargeting('env', window.adobeDataLayer?.[0]?.site?.environment || 'n/a');


        googletag.defineSlot($GooglePublisherCategory, 'fluid', 'advert-leaderboard')
            .defineSizeMapping(sizes.leaderboard)
            .addService(googletag.pubads())
            .setCollapseEmptyDiv(true);

        if(window.innerWidth >= 992) { // Remove MPU ad units from mobile view (LIT-621710)
            googletag.defineSlot($GooglePublisherCategory, 'fluid', 'advert-rail-1')
                .defineSizeMapping(sizes.rail)
                .setTargeting('Position', 'advert-rail-1')
                .addService(googletag.pubads())
                .setCollapseEmptyDiv(true);

            googletag.defineSlot($GooglePublisherCategory, 'fluid', 'advert-rail-2')
                .defineSizeMapping(sizes.rail)
                .setTargeting('Position', 'advert-rail-2')
                .addService(googletag.pubads())
                .setCollapseEmptyDiv(true);
        }

		onDvtagReady();	// DoubleVerify (PERICLES-19717)

        if(isProgrammaticAdsEnabled) {
            // PERICLES-17135
            googletag.pubads().disableInitialLoad();
        }
		
        googletag.enableServices();
    });

    if (isProgrammaticAdsEnabled) {
        googletag.cmd.push(function () {
            apstag.fetchBids({timeout: 2000}, function (bids) {
                apstag.setDisplayBids();
                onDvtagReady(function () {
					setTimeout(() => {
						googletag.pubads().refresh();
					}, 300);
                })
            });
        });
    }
</script>

<!-- loading Javascript for using the persistence Service -->
    <script src="/wro/f5e7ffe6d6d1192a4defdde88dc425c93326c6ed~thiss-ds.js" nonce="9f011e09d1779ca5-SJC"></script>

    <script type="text/javascript" nonce="9f011e09d1779ca5-SJC">

        function getParameter(parameterName) {
            let result = null,
                tmp = [];
            location.search
                .substr(1)
                .split("&")
                .forEach(function (item) {
                    tmp = item.split("=");
                    if (tmp[0] === parameterName) result = tmp[1];
                });
            return result;
        }

        var SeamlessAccessService = {
            spUrl: "/action/ssostart",
            returnURL: window.location.href,
            my_context: 'seamlessaccess.org',
            queryParamsDelimiter: encodeURIComponent('?'),
            _persistenceService: null,
            _getPersistenceService: function () {
                if (!this._persistenceService) {
                    this._persistenceService = new thiss.PersistenceService('https://service.seamlessaccess.org/ps/');
                }
                return this._persistenceService;
            },

            getWAYFLessUrl: function (entityId) {
                let redirectURL = getParameter('redirectUri');
                if(redirectURL == null){
                    redirectURL = encodeURIComponent(this.returnURL);
                }
                if(redirectURL.indexOf('saml_referrer') < 0){
                    redirectURL += encodeURIComponent(((redirectURL.indexOf(this.queryParamsDelimiter) !== -1) ? '&' : '?') + 'saml_referrer');
                }
                return this.spUrl + "?idp=" + encodeURIComponent(entityId)
                    + "&redirectUri=" + redirectURL;
            },
            retrieveEntity: function () {
                var self = this;
                var ps = this._getPersistenceService();

                return new Promise(function (resolve, reject) {
                    ps.entities(self.my_context)
                        .then(function (res) {
                            // get the results
                            var results = res.data;
                            return resolve(results);
                        }, function (err) {
                            // failed
                            reject('retrieve entity error: ' + err);
                        });
                });
            },
            updateEntity: function (entitydata) {
                var self = this;
                var ps = this._getPersistenceService();

                var jsonObj = {};
                try {
                    //try to parse it, if it is a json string
                    jsonObj = JSON.parse(entitydata.val());
                } catch (e) {
                    jsonObj = entitydata;
                }
                return new Promise(function (resolve, reject) {
                    ps.update(self.my_context, jsonObj)
                        .then(function (res) {
                            // Process the results
                            var ssoInstitutions = res.data;
                            return resolve(ssoInstitutions);
                        }, function (err) {
                            // failed
                            console.log('failed to update the local storage duo to: ' + err);
                            reject(err);
                        });
                })
            },
            removeEntity: function (entityId) {
                var self = this;
                var persistService = this._getPersistenceService();

                return new Promise(function (resolve) {
                    persistService.remove(self.my_context, entityId)
                        .then(function (res) {
                            return resolve(res);
                        }, function (err) {
                            // failed
                            console.log('failed to remove from local storage duo to: ' + err);
                        });
                })
            },
            getAccessibleEntityStatus: function (doi, entityId, entityTitle) {
                return new Promise(function (resolve) {
                    $.ajax({
                        type: 'POST',
                        url: '/action/seamlessAccess',
                        accept: {
                            text: "application/json"
                        },
                        data: {doi: doi, entityid: entityId, entityTitle: entityTitle, eventGroupKey: '5a595dc6-5ce2-4b6f-8aa2-de469adf1355'}

                    }).done(function (status) {
                        return resolve(status);
                    });
                });
            },
            getEntitiesWithAllInfo: function (doi) {
                let self = this,
                    arrayOfPromises = [];
                return new Promise(function (resolve, reject) {
                    self.retrieveEntity().then(function (res) {
                        let allSsoInstitutions = res;
                        for (let i in allSsoInstitutions) {
                            let ssoInstitution = allSsoInstitutions[i];
                            arrayOfPromises.push(new Promise(function (resolve) {
                                self.getAccessibleEntityStatus(doi, ssoInstitution.entity.entityID, ssoInstitution.entity.title)
                                    .then(function (entityStatus) {
                                        resolve({
                                            entityID: ssoInstitution.entity.entityID,
                                            entityTitle: ssoInstitution.entity.title,
                                            entityStatusObj: entityStatus,
                                            entitySsoUrl: self.getWAYFLessUrl(ssoInstitution.entity.entityID)
                                        });
                                    }, function (err) {
                                        console.log('failed to get the entity status: ' + err);
                                    });
                            }));
                        }
                        Promise.all(arrayOfPromises)
                            .then(function (finalResponse) {
                                resolve(finalResponse);
                            });

                    }).catch(function (err) {
                        reject(err);
                    })
                });
            }
        };

        window.addEventListener('load', function () {
            document.querySelectorAll(".sso-institution").forEach(function (institution) {
                var entityId = institution.getAttribute('data-entityid');
                var entityName = institution.getAttribute('data-name');
                var entitydata = {
                    entityID: entityId,
                    title: entityName
                };
                institution.addEventListener("click", function (e) {
                    e.preventDefault();
                    var self = this;
                    SeamlessAccessService.updateEntity(entitydata).then(function (res) {
                        console.log(res);
                        window.location.href = self.href;
                    }).catch(function (err){
                        window.location.href = self.href;
                    });
                });
            });
        });

    </script>

        (function() {
            function riskifiedBeaconLoad() {
                var store_domain = 'https://advanced.onlinelibrary.wiley.com';
                var session_id = '493C072DD1F3E3885D4883D4841886F1';
                var url = ('https:' == document.location.protocol ? 'https://' : 'http://')
                    + "beacon.riskified.com?shop=" + store_domain + "&sid=" + session_id;
                var s = document.createElement('script');
                s.type = 'text/javascript';
                s.async = true;
                s.src = url;
                var x = document.getElementsByTagName('script')[0];
                x.parentNode.insertBefore(s, x);
            }
            if (window.attachEvent)
                window.attachEvent('onload', riskifiedBeaconLoad)
            else
                window.addEventListener('load', riskifiedBeaconLoad, false);
        })();

   <div class="abstract-group ">
      <section class="article-section article-section__abstract" lang="en" data-lang="en" lang-name="English" id="section-1-en">
         <h2 id="d171122259" class="article-section__header section__title main abstractlang_en main">Abstract</h2>
         <div class="article-section__content en main">
            
            <p>The advancement of message RNA (mRNA) -based immunotherapies for cancer is highly dependent on the effective delivery of RNA (Ribonucleic) payloads using ionizable lipid nanoparticles (LNPs). However, the clinical application of these therapies is hindered by variable mRNA expression among different cancer types and the risk of systemic toxicity. The transient expression profile of mRNA further complicates this issue, necessitating frequent dosing and thus increasing the potential for adverse effects. Addressing these challenges, a high-throughput combinatorial method is utilized to synthesize and screen LNPs that efficiently deliver circular RNA (circRNA) to lung tumors. The lead LNP, H1L1A1B3, demonstrates a fourfold increase in circRNA transfection efficiency in lung cancer cells over ALC-0315, the industry-standard LNPs, while providing potent immune activation. A single intratumoral injection of H1L1A1B3 LNPs, loaded with circRNA encoding interleukin-12 (IL-12), induces a robust immune response in a Lewis lung carcinoma model, leading to marked tumor regression. Immunological profiling of treated tumors reveals substantial increments in CD45<sup>+</sup> leukocytes and enhances infiltration of CD8<sup>+</sup> T cells, underscoring the ability of H1L1A1B3 LNPs to modulate the tumor microenvironment favorably. These results highlight the potential of tailored LNP platforms to advance RNA drug delivery for cancer therapy, broadening the prospects for RNA immunotherapeutics.</p>
            </div>
      </section>
   </div>
   <div class="pb-dropzone" data-pb-dropzone="below-abstract-group"><!-- Empty dropzone --></div>
   
   <section class="article-section article-section__full">
      <section class="article-section__content" id="adma202400307-sec-0010">
         
         <h2 class="article-section__title section__title section1" id="adma202400307-sec-0010-title">1 Introduction</h2>
         
         <p>Cancer immunotherapy, including immune checkpoint blockade, represents a revolutionary approach in oncology, harnessing the body's immune system to identify and combat malignant cells.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0001" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0001_R_d171122243e504" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 1 - Nat. Rev. Clin. Oncol.">1</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Its efficacy, however, varies across different cancer types, largely owing to the immunosuppressive nature of the tumor microenvironment (TME).<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0002" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0002_R_d171122243e509" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 2 - J. Hematol. Oncol.">2</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> The TME can hinder the activity and infiltration of key immune cells like natural killer T (NKT) cells and cytotoxic T lymphocytes, which are essential for the success of many immunotherapies.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0003" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0003_R_d171122243e514" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 3 - Cancer Pathog. Ther. BMB Rep.">3</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Consequently, treatments such as immune checkpoint blockade alone might not be sufficient for durable tumor inhibition or elimination. Addressing this issue requires a strategic remodeling of the TME, which often involves reversing its immunosuppressive state and inducing inflammatory immune mechanisms.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0004" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0004_R_d171122243e520" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 4 - Science Sci. Transl. Med.">4</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> To this end, cytokines have proven effective in modulating T-cell activation and differentiation, thereby enhancing the efficacy of cancer immunotherapy.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0005" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0005_R_d171122243e525" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 5 - Cell Mol. Immunol.">5</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> IL-12 in particular has emerged as a promising candidate due to its ability to activate multiple immune subpopulations.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0006" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0006_R_d171122243e531" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 6 - Nat. Rev. Clin. Oncol. J. Exp. Med.">6</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> It can strongly stimulate antigen-presenting cells (APCs) and other cytokines, primarily interferon-gamma (IFN-γ), bolstering responses of T-cells and NKT cells, which in turn strengthens anti-tumor immunity in vivo.</p>
         
         <p>Driven by the remarkable anti-tumor efficacy of IL-12 in preclinical studies, multiple IL-12 therapies have entered clinical trials for cancer immunotherapy. However, the severe toxicity associated with systemic administration has led to numerous early trial terminations.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0007" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0007_R_d171122243e539" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 7 - Front. Immunol. Nat. Biomed. Eng. Blood">7</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> As a result, many strategies are currently being explored to mitigate toxicity linked with systemic IL-12 exposure.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0008" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0008_R_d171122243e544" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 8 - Pharmacol. Ther.">8</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> One such approach under clinical evaluation (NCT03946800, NCT03871348) is the intratumoral administration of IL-12.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0009" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0009_R_d171122243e549" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 9 - Nat. Rev. Clin. Oncol. Clin. Cancer Res.">9</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> For instance, Dong et&nbsp;al. demonstrated intratumoral delivery of IL-12 mRNA encapsulated in lipid nanoparticles (LNPs) promotes immune cell infiltration and inhibits tumor growth in murine melanoma models across six doses.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0010" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0010_R_d171122243e555" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 10 - J. Controlled Release">10</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> In an effort to address the transient nature of mRNA expression and reduce the frequency of dosing, Weiss et al. reported that a single intratumoral dose of an LNP-encapsulated self-replicating mRNA encoding IL-12 can remodel the TME and stimulate systemic anti-tumor immunity, resulting in effective tumor regression in murine melanoma models.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0011" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0011_R_d171122243e560" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 11 - Nat Cancer">11</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Despite its benefits over traditional mRNA, self-replicating mRNA encounters several challenges, including instability and reduced in vivo delivery efficiency due to its large size,<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0012" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0012_R_d171122243e566" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 12 - NPJ Vaccines">12</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> along with relatively high immunogenicity due to its poor compatibility with base modification.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0013" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0013_R_d171122243e571" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 13 - NPJ Vaccines">13</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup></p>
         
         <p>In addressing the limitations of conventional RNA therapies, circular RNA (circRNA) has emerged as a promising alternative. CircRNA, a covalently closed loop of RNA produced by intron back-splicing in eukaryotic cells, possesses diverse protein-coding and noncoding functions.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0014" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0014_R_d171122243e578" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 14 - Cell Signal Transduction Targeted Ther.">14</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Its unique ring-like endless structure renders it resistant to degradation by nucleic acid exonucleases, thereby enhancing its shelf life and biological stability.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0015" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0015_R_d171122243e583" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 15 - Nucleic Acids Res. Nature">15</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Thanks to this enhanced stability, circRNA exhibits a significantly extended in vivo half-life—up to 2.5 times longer than linear mRNA.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0016" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0016_R_d171122243e588" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 16 - Nat. Rev. Genet.">16</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Additionally, purified circRNA has been shown to induce lower innate immune activation compared to unmodified mRNA.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0017" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0017_R_d171122243e594" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 17 - Mol. Cell">17</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Notably, unlike linear mRNA, circRNA does not require modified nucleosides, 5′ cap analogs, and 3′poly-A tails to improve its safety, translation, and stability, positioning it as a cost-effective option.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0018" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0018_R_d171122243e599" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 18 - Nat. Rev. Mol. Cell Biol. J. Controlled Release">18</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> The potential of circular RNA (circRNA) in cancer immunotherapy is evident, yet its successful application hinges on the effectiveness of delivery systems. With the approval of RNA therapeutics like ONPATTRO (Patisiran) and the widespread use of COVID-19 mRNA vaccines, LNP has become a leading non-viral approach for mRNA delivery.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0019" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0019_R_d171122243e605" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 19 - Adv. Sci.">19</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup></p>
         
         <p>LNPs contain multiple compositions, among which the ionizable lipid component is crucial for effective delivery. This key component significantly influences LNP functionality, including RNA encapsulation, toxicity mitigation, and facilitating endosomal escape.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0020" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0020_R_d171122243e612" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 20 - Small Methods Nat. Rev. Mater.">20</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Structural modifications to ionizable lipids markedly impact RNA delivery efficiency.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0021" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0021_R_d171122243e617" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 21 - Nat. Biomed. Eng. Angew. Chem., Int. Ed.">21</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> However, it is noteworthy that no single ionizable lipid structure is universally applicable for all purposes. LNPs designed for liver or muscle targets may not be equally effective in other tissues. This principle extends to mRNA delivery in tumor tissues via LNPs, which present unique challenges due to properties like low pH and hypoxia, distinguishing them from healthy tissues.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0022" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0022_R_d171122243e622" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 22 - Angew. Chem., Int. Ed. Cancer Cell">22</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> It has been shown that hypoxic cells show reduced expression levels of mRNA delivered via LNPs compared to cells in normoxic conditions.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0023" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0023_R_d171122243e628" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 23 - J. Am. Chem. Soc.">23</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Moreover, tumor cell properties vary among different cancer types,<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0024" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0024_R_d171122243e633" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 24 - Sci. Adv.">24</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> suggesting the need to customize LNPs to individual cancer types for optimal RNA transfection in tumor tissues, thereby maximizing the efficacy of RNA-based cancer immunotherapy.</p>
         
         <p>In this study, we have employed a high-throughput approach to rapidly synthesize and evaluate a diverse library of ionizable lipids, specifically for circRNA delivery to lung tumors. Our leading candidate, H1L1A1B3, when incorporated into LNPs, demonstrates a remarkable increase in transfection efficiency of circRNA in the LLC1 lung cancer cell line. This efficiency is four times greater than the industry standard LNP, ALC-0315, utilized in Pfizer/BioNTech's COVID-19 mRNA vaccine. Additionally, H1L1A1B3 LNPs are notable for their ability to activate the NF-κB/IRF immune pathways. A singular intratumoral administration of these LNPs, carrying IL-12 circRNA, has been shown to significantly boost immune cell infiltration in the tumor milieu and induce substantial tumor regression in a murine LLC1 tumor model. Notably, H1L1A1B3-LNPs have also demonstrated efficacy in delivering circRNA directly to the lungs. A single intratracheal dose of IL-12 circRNA encapsulated in H1L1A1B3-LNPs effectively curtailed tumor growth in an orthotopic HKP1 lung tumor model. Collectively, this research not only pioneers a new circRNA-based immunotherapy for lung cancer but also introduces a tailored approach for LNP customization to specific cancer types, paving the way for advancements in personalized cancer nanomedicine.</p>
         </section>
      <section class="article-section__content" id="adma202400307-sec-0020">
         
         <h2 class="article-section__title section__title section1" id="adma202400307-sec-0020-title">2 Results</h2>
         
         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="adma202400307-sec-0030">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="adma202400307-sec-0030-title">2.1 Development of the Customizable LNP for circRNA Delivery in Lung Cancer Cells through Combinatorial Ionizable Lipid Library</h3>
            
            <p>Given the time-consuming, labor-intensive, and high-risk nature of ionizable lipid synthesis, the goal of many researchers is to rapidly generate large quantities of these lipids for in vitro screening through combinatorial chemistry.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0025" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0025_R_d171122243e655" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 25 - J. Mater. Chem. B">25</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Combinatorial chemistry offers significant advantages over traditional synthesis methods, including reduced time, reduced cost, and the ability to perform high-throughput screening of ionizable lipids. Hence, the Ugi four-component reaction (4CR-Ugi) was utilized here for efficiently generating an ionizable lipid library for circRNA delivery (<b>Figure</b> <a href="#adma202400307-fig-0001"><b>1</b>a</a>).<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0026" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0026_R_d171122243e666" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 26 - Angew. Chem., Int. Ed.">26</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> The 96 chemically diverse ionizable lipids produced via the Ugi reaction were synthesized based on our previous work (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#adma202400307-fig-0001">1b</a>).<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0027" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0027_R_d171122243e675" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 27 - Nat. Biomed. Eng.">27</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Then, the commonly used pipette mixing approach was adopted to prepare LNPs for high throughput screening in Lewis lung carcinoma (LLC1) cells. These synthesized ionizable lipids were formulated into LNPs using a platform formulation of 1,2-dioleoyl-sn-glycerol-3-phosphoethanolamine (DOPE), cholesterol, and 1,2-dimyristoyl-sn-glycero-3-methoxypolyethylene glycol (PEG-DMG2000) at molar ratios of 50:10:38.5:1.5, respectively. Firefly luciferase (FLuc) circRNA was encapsulated into the LNPs at a weight ratio of 7.5 ionizable lipid to 1 circRNA. This LNP formulation was used to evaluate the circRNA delivery efficiency mediated by each ionizable lipid candidate (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#adma202400307-fig-0001">1c</a>). Based on the screening results, H1L1A1B3 was identified as the lead ionizable lipid candidate for effective circRNA delivery in LLC1 cells and was utilized for further formulation optimization studies (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#adma202400307-fig-0001">1d</a> and Supporting Information).</p>
            
            <section class="article-section__inline-figure">
               <figure class="figure" id="adma202400307-fig-0001"><a target="_blank" href="/cms/asset/2a71b0c0-123b-440c-8b71-033f3c798488/adma202400307-fig-0001-m.jpg"><picture>

         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="adma202400307-sec-0040">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="adma202400307-sec-0040-title">2.2 Evaluation of Kinetic and Immunostimulatory LNP for IL-12 circRNA Delivery</h3>
            
            <p>First, we sought to test whether LNPs encapsulating nucleic acids could be used for IL-12 expression. For producing circRNA cargoes, we utilized a previously reported optimized splicing method called the permuted intron-exon (PIE) approach to synthesize the IL-12 circRNA precursor with a CVB3 Internal Ribosomal Entry Site (IRES) (<b>Figure</b> <a href="#adma202400307-fig-0002"><b>2</b>a</a>).<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0017" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0017_R_d171122243e720" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 17 - Mol. Cell">17</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> To achieve high-purity IL-12 circRNA, we employed a high-performance liquid chromatography (HPLC) purification method (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#adma202400307-fig-0002">2b</a>). The high-purity IL-12 circRNA was obtained from the second half of the HPLC main peak, confirmed through agarose-gel RNA electrophoresis (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#adma202400307-fig-0002">2c</a>). This step ensured the isolation of the target IL-12 circRNA in its most refined form, facilitating its subsequent use in our experiments. As a control, we synthesized capped and tailed IL-12 mRNA (Plasmid #108665 from add gene). This mRNA differed from IL-12 circRNA by its presence of a cap and tail and absence of IRES and remnants of PIE splicing elements, with no differences in transcriptional regulatory elements or untranslated regions (UTRs).</p>
            
            <section class="article-section__inline-figure">
               <figure class="figure" id="adma202400307-fig-0002"><a target="_blank" href="/cms/asset/56e5f95b-9917-43d1-861d-8b07fad263ff/adma202400307-fig-0002-m.jpg"><picture>
                        <source srcset="/cms/asset/56e5f95b-9917-43d1-861d-8b07fad263ff/adma202400307-fig-0002-m.jpg" media="(min-width: 1650px)"></source><img class="figure__image" src="/cms/asset/adada0bd-1874-4d1d-8a82-e0e3144f7570/adma202400307-fig-0002-m.png" data-lg-src="/cms/asset/56e5f95b-9917-43d1-861d-8b07fad263ff/adma202400307-fig-0002-m.jpg" alt="Details are in the caption following the image" title="Details are in the caption following the image" loading="lazy"></picture></a><figcaption class="figure__caption">
                     <div class="figure__caption__header"><strong class="figure__title">Figure 2<span style="font-weight:normal"></span></strong><div class="figure-extra"><a href="#" class="open-figure-link">Open in figure viewer</a><a href="/action/downloadFigures?id=adma202400307-fig-0002&amp;partId=&amp;doi=10.1002%2Fadma.202400307" class="ppt-figure-link"><i aria-hidden="true" class="icon-Icon_Download"></i><span>PowerPoint</span></a></div>
                     </div>
                     <div class="figure__caption figure__caption-text">Production and characterization of IL-12 circRNA and H1L1A1B3 LNPs. a) Schematic diagram showing IL-12 circRNA permuted intron-exon construct design and mechanism of splicing. b) HPLC chromatogram of an unpurified splicing reaction. c) Agarose gel of purified IL-12 circRNA fractions. d) Particle size of H1L1A1B3 LNPs with IL-12 mRNA and circRNA. e) Characterization of lipid H1L1A1B3 LNPs containing IL-12 mRNA and circRNA, including size, polydispersity index (PDI), zeta potential (ZP), and encapsulation efficiency (EE). f) The expression levels of IL-12p70 were measured using ELISA on the supernatants of LLC1 and HKP1 cells that were transfected with IL-12 circRNA LNPs. (Untreated and naked circRNA were considered as negative controls). g,h) The IL-12p70 expression levels of IL-12 circRNA and IL-12 mRNA encapsulated in H1L1A1B3 LNPs (200&nbsp;ng per well in a 24-well plate) in LLC1 and HKP1 cells were measured by ELISA. FLuc-mRNA and FLuc-circRNA were considered as scrambled RNA for the control groups. i,j) Secretion of SEAP and IRF activity folds changes after incubating with empty H1L1A1B3 LNPs and H1A1B3-Cyc without cargo for 24&nbsp;h in NF-κB and IRF-induced interferon regulatory factor (IRF) activation reporter monocytes (THP1-Dual Cells, <i>n</i> = 3 biologically independent experiments per group), compared with untreated cells. k) IL-12 circRNA LNP delivery and translation processes in tumor cells. l) Two different administration routes against two mouse tumor models. m) Mechanisms for IL-12 circRNA remodeling of the tumor microenvironment and tumor immunotherapy. The figures were created by BioRenders.com. Statistical significance was analyzed by the two-tailed Student's <i>t</i>-test. * = <i>p</i>-value &lt; 0.05, ** = <i>p</i>-value &lt; 0.01, *** = <i>p</i>-value &lt; 0.005, **** = <i>p</i>-value &lt; 0.001. Data are presented as mean ± SD.</div>
                  </figcaption>
               </figure>
            </section>
            <p>Using the lead ionizable lipid H1L1A1B3 identified previously, we encapsulated IL-12 mRNA and circRNA into LNPs based on previous references and aforementioned formulation optimization to enable direct comparison (Table <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S1</a>, Supporting Information).<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0027" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0027_R_d171122243e771" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 27 - Nat. Biomed. Eng.">27</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> This standardized approach ensured the LNPs containing mRNA or circRNA payloads were comparable for subsequent evaluation. Notably, measurements of the physical properties showed no significant differences in particle size, zeta potential, encapsulation efficiency, and polydispersity index between the H1L1A1B3 LNP formulations containing either IL-12 mRNA or circRNA payloads (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#adma202400307-fig-0002">2d,e</a> and Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S1</a>, Supporting Information). This consistency allowed for a fair comparison of the intrinsic differences between mRNA and circRNA, as the differences in LNPs used for their delivery were minimal.</p>
            
            <p>To delve deeper into the expression of IL-12 p70 in various lung cancer cell lines treated with these LNPs, LLC1 and HKP1 cells were subjected to LNP treatment for 24 h. An ELISA assay was then used to measure the levels of IL-12 p70 produced by the LLC1 and HKP1 cells. In comparison to the benchmark ALC-0315, H1L1A1B3 LNPs demonstrated the highest transfection potency in both LLC1 and HKP1 cells (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#adma202400307-fig-0002">2f</a>). To assess the kinetics of IL-12 circRNA expression in comparison to IL-12 mRNA, supernatants were collected from IL-12 circRNA- and IL-12 mRNA-treated LLC1 and HKP1 lung cancer cells for 7 consecutive days. The findings revealed that IL-12 circRNA demonstrated a considerably delayed decay when compared to the expression of IL-12 mRNA (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#adma202400307-fig-0002">2g,h</a>). To provide a more intuitive representation of the decay of circRNA and linear mRNA in vivo, we encapsulated FLuc circRNA and FLuc mRNA into LNPs and administered them to mice intramuscularly. The results were consistent with in vitro experiments (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S2a</a>, Supporting Information). Specifically, although the initial average bioluminescence (BLI) intensity of FLuc mRNA was slightly higher than that of FLuc circRNA, the decay rate of the signal intensity of FLuc mRNA was much faster than that of FLuc circRNA (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S2b</a>, Supporting Information). These results indicated that the window of circRNA expression in vivo is greater than that of mRNA under the same circumstances, which could help to induce a sustained humoral immune response. These data suggest that IL-12 circRNA encapsulated in H1L1A1B3 LNPs are capable of producing sustained circRNA expression in vitro and in vivo.</p>
            
            <p>We also sought to characterize the effect of empty LNPs on the induction of immune responses in lung cancer cell lines. Previous studies have reported that ionizable lipid-containing cyclic groups can enhance immune stimulation by significantly increasing IFN-γ secretion.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0028" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0028_R_d171122243e800" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 28 - Nat. Biotechnol.">28</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Interestingly, the lead ionizable lipid we identified through high throughput screening, H1L1A1B3, also incorporates the cyclic group (cyclohexyl isocyanide) in its structure. Hence, to gain insight into the involvement of H1L1A1B3 LNP without cargo in the innate immune sensing pathway, NF-κB-SEAP and IRF-Lucia luciferase reporter monocytes were used to investigate the immune activation pathway induced by immunostimulatory lipid H1L1A1B3. To further investigate the effect of the cyclic group on immune activation, we synthesized another ionizable lipid, H1A1B3-Cyc, which contains a five-membered ring structure and keeps the amine head and tail as the same as H1L1A1B3. In addition, two industry benchmark LNPs for efficient mRNA delivery containing commercially available ionizable lipids DLin-MC3-DMA (MC3) and ALC-0315 were included as controls. The results showed that both H1L1A1B3 and H1A1B3-Cyc LNPs without cargo significantly induced NF-κB activation and triggered the IRF pathway when compared to benchmarks (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#adma202400307-fig-0002">2i,j</a>). The innate immune system can induce type I IFN and cytokine (TNF-α) production via these two pathways. While both five-membered and six-membered rings exhibited immune activation capabilities in this context, it is important to note that not all ring structures necessarily share this property.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0029" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0029_R_d171122243e808" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 29 - Nat. Nanotechnol.">29</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Hence, exploring ionizable lipids for their immune activation potential through combinatorial chemistry strategies appears promising. In summary, these data suggest that H1L1A1B3 LNPs without cargo have properties to activate innate immune pathways.</p>
            
            <p>Building on our findings, we sought to evaluate the therapeutic potential of the engineered gene delivery system containing the customized lipid formulation and IL-12 circRNA payload in lung cancer models. The delivery of IL-12 circRNA LNPs through two distinct administration routes, by intratumoral and intratracheal injection, was evaluated in two different mouse lung cancer models (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#adma202400307-fig-0002">2k</a>). These LNPs are internalized by cells and translated into the IL-12 cytokine (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#adma202400307-fig-0002">2l</a>). As illustrated in Figure&nbsp;<a href="#adma202400307-fig-0002">2m</a>, this in turn can reshape the TME, thereby recruiting and activating immune cells and associated stromal components, restoring the inherent anti-tumor capabilities of the immune system.</p>
            </section>
         
         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="adma202400307-sec-0050">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="adma202400307-sec-0050-title">2.3 Improving Lewis Lung Cancer Immunotherapy through Intratumoral Injection of IL-12 circRNA LNPs</h3>
            
            <p>Immune checkpoint inhibitor (ICI) therapy is currently the most commonly used immunotherapy regimen and has been approved for the clinical treatment of a variety of tumors, with breakthroughs in the treatment of lung cancer.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0030" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0030_R_d171122243e835" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 30 - J. Thorac. Oncol.">30</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> However, due to inherent tumor heterogeneity and the intricacies of the TME, ICI monotherapy is insufficient to control tumor proliferation.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0031" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0031_R_d171122243e840" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 31 - Cell Res.">31</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> One possible explanation for the inability of ICI alone to control tumor growth is that simply lifting immunosuppression may not restore sufficient anti-tumor immunity, and an increase in T cells or tumor-killing cytokines is needed to induce anti-tumor immunity.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0032" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0032_R_d171122243e845" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 32 - J. Biomed. Sci.">32</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Mechanistically, the binding of IL-12 to T cells causes a cascade of phenotypic changes, including proinflammatory cytokine IFN-γ production, which can subsequently lead to anti-angiogenic effects and tumor injury mediated by IFN-γ, potentially inducing tumor regression.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0033" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0033_R_d171122243e851" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 33 - Clin. Cancer Res.">33</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Given this, we hypothesized that IL-12 could enhance the efficacy of anti-PD-L1 treatment.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0034" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0034_R_d171122243e856" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 34 - Sci. Immunol.">34</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Consequently, we investigated whether the combined therapy of IL-12 circRNA LNPs could augment the effectiveness of ICIs (<b>Figure</b> <a href="#adma202400307-fig-0003"><b>3</b>a</a>). Mice treated solely with systemic application of anti-PD-L1 did not exhibit significant regression in LLC1 tumors (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#adma202400307-fig-0003">3b</a>). Intriguingly, even a single administration of IL-12 circRNA LNPs alone into established LLC tumors was sufficient to delay tumor progression. Notably, at the treatment endpoint, the combined therapy of ICI and cancer immunotherapy demonstrated the ability to induce robust tumor regression. Importantly, a related study demonstrated that the doubling time for a subcutaneous tumor derived from LLC1 cells was 23&nbsp;h for a tumor mass of about 0.1&nbsp;g and 40&nbsp;h for a tumor mass of about 1&nbsp;g.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0035" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0035_R_d171122243e871" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 35 - Cancer Lett. Am. J. Cancer Res.">35</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> In this study, similar results were observed, with the tumor tissue of the untreated group proliferating rapidly and significantly impacting body weight compared to the treated group. Correspondingly, mice in the combined treatment group did not experience significant weight loss or weight gain due to tumor growth compared to the anti-PD-L1 monotherapy group (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#adma202400307-fig-0003">3c</a>). Thus, we speculate that the dramatic difference in body weight observed between the untreated and treated groups was influenced by the disparity in tumor progression rates between the arms of the study. Simultaneously, the evidence also substantiates the safety of IL-12 for cancer immunotherapy when utilizing the customized LNP platform.</p>
            
            <section class="article-section__inline-figure">
               <figure class="figure" id="adma202400307-fig-0003"><a target="_blank" href="/cms/asset/49ab2a43-75b7-4822-956d-ce9a38b02495/adma202400307-fig-0003-m.jpg"><picture>
                        <source srcset="/cms/asset/49ab2a43-75b7-4822-956d-ce9a38b02495/adma202400307-fig-0003-m.jpg" media="(min-width: 1650px)"></source><img class="figure__image" src="/cms/asset/46625d01-5518-4ba4-9636-36aa08e65a0e/adma202400307-fig-0003-m.png" data-lg-src="/cms/asset/49ab2a43-75b7-4822-956d-ce9a38b02495/adma202400307-fig-0003-m.jpg" alt="Details are in the caption following the image" title="Details are in the caption following the image" loading="lazy"></picture></a><figcaption class="figure__caption">
                     <div class="figure__caption__header"><strong class="figure__title">Figure 3<span style="font-weight:normal"></span></strong><div class="figure-extra"><a href="#" class="open-figure-link">Open in figure viewer</a><a href="/action/downloadFigures?id=adma202400307-fig-0003&amp;partId=&amp;doi=10.1002%2Fadma.202400307" class="ppt-figure-link"><i aria-hidden="true" class="icon-Icon_Download"></i><span>PowerPoint</span></a></div>
                     </div>
                     <div class="figure__caption figure__caption-text">Intratumoral dosing of IL-12 circRNA LNP with anti-PD-L1 antibody effectively inhibits LLC1 tumor growth. a) Schematic of LLC1 tumor inoculation and IL-12 circRNA LNPs with antibody anti-PD-L1 for cancer immunotherapy. b) Tumor growth of C57BL/6J mice (<i>n</i> = 5 per group) inoculated with 1 × 10<sup>6</sup> LLC1 cells and then treated with PBS, IL-12 circRNA LNPs and anti-PD-L1 antibody injection on day 7 (<i>n</i> = 5 per group, 5&nbsp;µg IL-12 circRNA LNPs per mouse, 200&nbsp;µg anti- PD-L1 per mouse). FLuc-circRNA was considered as scrambled circRNA for the control groups. Tumor size was measured twice per week. c) Changes in body weight (mean ± SD) over time post-tumor inoculation. d) Representative flow cytometry quantification CD45<sup>+</sup> leukocyte images in tumor. e) Percentage of CD45<sup>+</sup> leucocytes in tumor. The number of tumoral CD8<sup>+</sup> T cells, CD4<sup>+</sup> T cells, NKT cells, granulocytes, monocytes, and the ratio of CD8<sup>+</sup> T cell/Tregs in tumors. Analyses by flow cytometry, bars are mean ±SD. f) Representative immunofluorescence staining of CD8/IFN-γ killer T cells within tumor regions of untreated or IL-12 circRNA LNPs treated mice. CD8 staining is shown as red; IFN-γ is shown as green; counterstain is DAPI (blue) in the merged image shown at the bottom (<i>n</i> = 5 biologically independent mice per group). g) Representative immunofluorescence staining of CD31 within tumor regions of anti-PD-L1 monotherapy or IL-12 circRNA LNPs combined with anti-PD-L1 treated mice. Shown is staining for nucleus (DAPI, blue) and CD31 (red); the merged image is shown at right (<i>n</i> = 5 biologically independent mice per group). Statistical significance was analyzed by the two-tailed Student's <i>t</i>-test. * = <i>p</i>-value &lt; 0.05, ** = <i>p</i>-value &lt; 0.01, *** = <i>p</i>-value &lt; 0.005, **** = <i>p</i>-value &lt; 0.001. Data are presented as mean ± SD.</div>
                  </figcaption>
               </figure>
            </section>
            <p>As we mentioned before, IL-12 plays an important role in promoting the proliferation of T cells and NKT cells, as well as stimulating the production of IFN-γ.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0036" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0036_R_d171122243e934" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 36 - Curr. Immunol. Rev.">36</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> To test the effect of IL-12 circRNA LNPs combined with anti-PD-L1 on the TME compared with anti-PD-L1 monotherapy, we performed flow cytometry analysis of treated LLC1 tumors. We found a relatively low proportion of CD45<sup>+</sup> leukocytes in LLC1 tumors with anti-PD-L1 dosing. However, a more than eightfold increase in the proportion of CD45<sup>+</sup> leukocytes in tumors of mice treated with anti-PD-L1 combined with IL-12 circRNA LNPs was observed, which was highly significant (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#adma202400307-fig-0003">3d</a>). Furthermore, the data demonstrated a significant increase in the infiltration of cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CD8<sup>+</sup> T cells) and CD4<sup>+</sup> T helper cells, both of which play crucial roles in mediating antitumor immune responses. In addition, we observed that the ratio of CD8<sup>+</sup> T cells to regulatory T cells (Treg) was increased. Meanwhile, the infiltration of other immune cells in the TME was also increased to varying degrees, including NKT cells, granulocytes, and monocytes (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#adma202400307-fig-0003">3e</a>). Tissue immunofluorescence analysis was then conducted to examine whether co-administration of IL-12 circRNA LNPs could enhance IFN-γ production in the TME. The results showed increased IFN-γ generation and signaling in tumors from animals that received combination treatment compared to single agent therapies, supporting our hypothesis that IL-12 circRNA could stimulate IFN-γ production in vivo. (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#adma202400307-fig-0003">3f</a>). Furthermore, the combined treatment of ICI and IL-12 led to a more pronounced regression of the vascular marker CD31 in tumor tissues compared to other treatment groups (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#adma202400307-fig-0003">3g</a>).<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0037" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0037_R_d171122243e963" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 37 - J. Immunol.">37</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> The provided data strongly suggest that IL-12, when combined with ICIs, can serve as a valuable adjunct to immunotherapy.</p>
            </section>
         
         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="adma202400307-sec-0060">
            
            <h3 class="article-section__sub-title section2" id="adma202400307-sec-0060-title">2.4 Intratracheal Administration of IL-12 circRNA LNPs Suppresses Kras-Driven NSCLC Progression</h3>
            
            <p>Current drug delivery strategies for lung cancer are mainly based on the systemic administration of oral or intravenous antitumor drugs, which usually reach the lungs at very low concentrations. However, systemic drug levels remain high and therefore carry the risk of toxicity and adverse effects.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0038" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0038_R_d171122243e977" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 38 - Environ. Int.">38</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> The direct exposure of the lungs to the environment through the vascular-rich trachea and dendritic branches of the bronchi presents an opportunity for even distribution and rapid absorption of administered drugs.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0039" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0039_R_d171122243e982" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 39 - Respir. Physiol.">39</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Transtracheal administration not only avoids hepatic first-pass metabolism, but the drug is not subject to rapid clearance in the circulation and high dosage exposure in off-target tissues.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0040" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0040_R_d171122243e987" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 40 - Chest Adv. Drug Delivery Rev.">40</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> More importantly, the lower drug dose required for intratracheal administration also avoids drug resistance due to higher drug dosage or more frequent administration.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0006" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0006_R_d171122243e993" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 6 - Nat. Rev. Clin. Oncol. J. Exp. Med.">6</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Multiple studies have demonstrated the feasibility of transtracheal and intratracheal delivery of LNPs.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0041" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0041_R_d171122243e998" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 41 - ACS Nano">41</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Hence, we evaluated whether intratracheal delivery of H1L1A1B3 LNP could induce regression of lung cancer (<b>Figure</b> <a href="#adma202400307-fig-0004"><b>4</b>a</a>). We utilized the HKP1 (Kras<sup>G12D</sup>p53<sup>−/−</sup>) orthotopic, immunocompetent, syngeneic preclinical model of NSCLC, previously shown to be similar to human adenocarcinoma. In this preclinical lung cancer disease model, lung tumor regression after the treatment can be visualized by BLI. Hence, on day 9 post-treatment we detected considerable tumors in the lungs of the mice by BLI, and the mice were randomly divided into treatment and non-treatment groups. IL-12 circRNA LNP was given to the mice in the treatment group through the trachea. Lung cancer growth was assessed every 5 days post-administration, and we found that lung cancers in untreated mice grew persistently, whereas lung tumor load was significantly reduced in IL-12 circRNA-treated mice (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#adma202400307-fig-0004">4b,c</a>). Moreover, untreated mice showed significant weight loss due to persistent lung cancer deterioration compared to mice subjected to IL-12 circRNA therapy (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#adma202400307-fig-0004">4d</a>). We observed increased serum levels of IL-12, IFN-γ, and other cytokines and chemokines in mice treated with IL-12 circRNA LNP, with the effects lasting for at least 5 days after intratracheal injection (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#adma202400307-fig-0004">4e,f</a>). However, serum levels of liver enzymes, including aspartate transaminase (AST) and alanine transaminase (ALT) remained within normal ranges (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S3</a>, Supporting Information). Mice were sacrificed 19 days after inoculation with HKP1 cells, and lung tissues were dissected for subsequent analysis. Flow cytometry analysis of cells extracted from lung tissues of IL-12 circRNA-treated mice showed an increase in levels of immune cells such as CD45<sup>+</sup> leukocytes, CD8<sup>+</sup> T cells, NKT cells, granulocytes, and monocytes, as well as an increased ratio of CD8<sup>+</sup> T cells to Treg cells, compared to the untreated group (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#adma202400307-fig-0004">4g,h</a>). The results of ELISA also showed that after intratracheal injection, the expression of IL-12 in lung tissue was significantly increased compared with the control group (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S4</a>, Supporting Information). Accordingly, we observed a significant reduction in tumor burden in IL-12 circRNA-dosed mice compared to the scrambled circRNA and untreated group. We quantified tumor burden histologically by the percentage of tumor tissue area in H&amp;E-stained sections of the lungs and appeared to obtain the same results (Figure&nbsp;<a href="#adma202400307-fig-0004">4i</a>). These results demonstrate that the delivery of IL-12 circRNA LNP through the trachea is effective for the purpose of controlling the growth of lung cancer.</p>
            
            <section class="article-section__inline-figure">
               <figure class="figure" id="adma202400307-fig-0004"><a target="_blank" href="/cms/asset/e4061429-f28a-4dd9-a342-f77c58603e58/adma202400307-fig-0004-m.jpg"><picture>
