                  </figcaption>
               </figure>
            </section>
         </section>
         </section>
      <section class="article-section__content" id="adma202400307-sec-0070">
         
         <h2 class="article-section__title section__title section1" id="adma202400307-sec-0070-title">3 Discussion</h2>
         
         <p>IL-12 serves as an effective inducer of cell-mediated immunity, stimulating the effector functions of activated T cells and NKT cells, thereby promoting the rejection of solid tumors.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0034" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0034_R_d171122243e1099" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 34 - Sci. Immunol.">34</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> However, systemic intravenous administration of recombinant IL-12 has previously demonstrated limited therapeutic efficacy and even triggered severe adverse effects.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0042" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0042_R_d171122243e1104" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 42 - Cell Death Differ.">42</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Currently, the optimal approach for administering recombinant IL-12 appears to involve cycles of either 5 consecutive days of intravenous infusion or 2 weeks of subcutaneous injection.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0011" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0011_R_d171122243e1109" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 11 - Nat Cancer">11</a>, <a href="#adma202400307-bib-0043" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0043_R_d171122243e1109" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 43 - OncoTargets Ther.">43</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Although these trials have established the maximum tolerated dose of IL-12 for different regimens, frequent systemic and subcutaneous administration of IL-12 has demonstrated dose-dependent adverse events due to binding to peripheral receptors on circulating immune cells.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0044" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0044_R_d171122243e1115" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 44 - Clin. Cancer Res. Gynecol. Oncol. Leuk. Res.">44</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> As a result, clinical trials of IL-12 protein-based therapies have failed.</p>
         
         <p>Promisingly, the local application of IL-12 has exhibited similar or even superior antitumor effects with favorable safety profiles, warranting further investigation.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0042" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0042_R_d171122243e1123" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 42 - Cell Death Differ.">42</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Numerous ongoing efforts have reported successful intratumoral delivery methods for IL-12 across various types of solid tumors. However, despite promising preliminary outcomes observed in strategies delivering IL-12 to cancer patients, immune therapy based on IL-12 has not yet received approval from the United States Food and Drug Administration (FDA). Currently, clinical trials are evaluating intratumoral injection of IL-12 encoding mRNA (NCT03946800, NCT03871348) to avoid the side effects of administering recombinant IL-12. However, these clinical trials based on mRNA required multiple injections to reach the therapeutic window due to the extremely short half-life of the mRNA. Hence, compared with direct IL-12 mRNA therapy, which has a shorter half-life, nanoparticle-mediated delivery of IL-12 circRNA enables the sustained expression of IL-12 in the tumor site, which results in a more potent and durable antitumor response.</p>
         
         <p>Consequently, this work aimed to address the limitations of current IL-12-based therapies for lung cancer by using a new approach. While intratumoral injection maximizes the delivery efficiency of LNPs to the tumor site, it is crucial to recognize that therapeutic outcomes are not solely determined by tumor accumulation efficiency. Other factors, including RNA transfection potency and the mechanism of action of the therapeutic payload, play vital roles. Hence, it is essential to tailor nanoparticles specifically for tumor cells to optimize therapeutic efficacy. Here, we advocated for the adoption of combinatorial chemistry to screen ionizable lipids, customizing them for circRNA delivery to lung cancer cells. Importantly, the results indicate that H1L1A1B31 is a dual-functional lipid capable of facilitating effective circRNA delivery and proactively stimulating innate immune activation upon injection. As NF-κB activation boosts the population of tumor-specific IFNγ-producing CD8+ T cells and is essential for tumor eradication, immune activation of NFκB assumes a significant anti-tumor function in preclinical research tumor models.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0045" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0045_R_d171122243e1131" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 45 - Nat. Rev. Immunol.">45</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> Given this situation, our research aimed to utilize the immunostimulatory potential of lipid H1L1A1B31 for the delivery of IL-12 circRNA to enhance the efficacy of tumor immunotherapy in multiple lung cancer mouse models. The study successfully combined immunostimulatory H1L1A1B31 LNPs with IL-12 circRNA, which exhibited better transfection potency than industry benchmark ALC-0315 LNPs. In addition, IL-12 circRNA LNPs also showed heightened stability and sustained IL-12 expression compared to mRNA. This makes them well-suited for improving the TME through continuous IL-12 expression. Additionally, to mitigate off-target toxicity associated with systemic administration, we explored the impact of intratumoral delivery on the effectiveness of tumor immunotherapy. Notably, robust tumor regression was achieved when IL-12 circRNA was combined with ICIs (anti-PD-L1), compared to other treatment groups.</p>
         
         <p>Furthermore, to investigate alternative administration routes for feasible tumor immunotherapy, we employed intratracheal administration in the HKP1 lung cancer mouse model. The findings consistently revealed that IL-12 circRNA-mediated tumor immunotherapy generates significant levels of cytokines, primarily IFN-γ, in the in situ lung cancer model, thus confirming IL-12's effectiveness as an inducer of antitumor immunity in preclinical models. Furthermore, IFN-γ released by T cells and NKT cells mediated a favorable immune phenotype within the TME, crucial for antitumor efficacy.</p>
         </section>
      <section class="article-section__content" id="adma202400307-sec-0080">
         
         <h2 class="article-section__title section__title section1" id="adma202400307-sec-0080-title">4 Conclusion</h2>
         
         <p>In conclusion, IL-12 has the potential to enhance tumor immunogenicity by modulating the TME and further harnessing immunogenicity through strategies such as using immunostimulatory LNPs and combination with ICIs. With this study, we have presented a novel LNP delivery system that can efficiently deliver cytokine-encoding circRNA to tumors. This novel system offers promising possibilities for advancing current lung cancer treatments by directly administering immunotherapy to the site of the cancer. Overall, our findings suggest this delivery platform is worth pursuing through further development as a potential new immunotherapeutic approach for lung cancer patients.</p>
         </section>
      <section class="article-section__content" id="adma202400307-sec-0090">
         
         <h2 class="article-section__title section__title section1" id="adma202400307-sec-0090-title">5 Experimental Section</h2>
         
         <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="adma202400307-sec-0100">
            
            <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="adma202400307-sec-0110">
               
               <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="adma202400307-sec-0120">
                  
                  <h4 class="article-section__sub-title section4" id="adma202400307-sec-0120-title"> Ionizable Lipid Library Synthesis</h4>
                  
                  <p>For ionizable lipid library synthesis, 4CR-Ugi was utilized to synthesize ionizable cationic lipids between amine groups (─NH<sub>2</sub>), aldehyde groups (─CHO), carboxylic acids (─COOH), and isocyanide groups (─NC). The amines, isocyanide, aldehyde tails, and carboxylic acids were obtained from TCI and Sigma Aldrich or synthesized following the methods described in the previous work. Briefly, the reactants were dissolved in ethanol and allowed to react in a 96-well PCR plate for 24 h, using a molar ratio of 1.1:1.1:1:1 (aldehyde: amine: isocyanide: carboxylic acid). The resulting crude products for high-throughput screening were prepared by removing ethanol without undergoing any further purification. The <sup>1</sup>H NMR and mass spectrum were provided in the supplementary information. Otherwise, the lipid was purified by flash column chromatography on a C-815 preparative chromatography system (BUCHI). The structure was confirmed by <sup>1</sup>H NMR spectrometry (400 or 800&nbsp;MHz, Bruker spectrometer) and Q Exactive HF-Orbitrap mass spectrometer (Thermo). Detailed chemical synthesis route is available in Supporting Information.</p>
                  </section>
               
               <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="adma202400307-sec-0130">
                  
                  <h4 class="article-section__sub-title section4" id="adma202400307-sec-0130-title"> RNA Design, Synthesis, and Purification</h4>
                  
                  <p>mRNA or circRNA precursors were synthesized by in vitro transcription from linearized plasmid DNA templates using the T7 High Yield RNA Synthesis Kit (New England Biolabs (NEB)). After in vitro transcription, the reaction was treated with DNase I (NEB) for 15&nbsp;min. After DNase I treatment, the RNA was cleaned up on a column using the RNA Cleanup Kit (NEB). The RNA was then heated to 65&nbsp;°C for 5&nbsp;min and immediately placed on ice for 5&nbsp;min, followed by capping with linear RNA using 2′-O-methyltransferase (NEB) and Vaccinia cap enzyme (NEB). Purification was performed again using the RNA Cleanup kit (NEB). Polyadenosine (PolyA) tails were added to the capped linear transcripts using <i>E. coli</i> PolyA polymerase (NEB) and the complete mRNA was purified. For circular RNA, GTP was mixed with magnesium-containing buffer (NEB) to a final concentration of 2 m<span class="smallCaps">m</span>, and the reaction was incubated for 15&nbsp;min at 55&nbsp;°C. RNA column purification was then performed. For HPLC analysis, 20 µL of RNA was heated at 65&nbsp;°C for 3&nbsp;min and then placed on ice for 5&nbsp;min. RNA was separated on a 30&nbsp;cm × 4.6&nbsp;mm size separation column (Millipore SEC-2000 Gel Filtration Column) on an Agilent 1260 with a particle size of 5 um. RNA was run in RNase-free TE buffer (10 m<span class="smallCaps">m</span> Tris, 1 m<span class="smallCaps">m</span> EDTA, pH:6) at a flow rate of 0.3&nbsp;mL&nbsp;min<sup>−1</sup>. RNA was detected by UV absorbance at 260&nbsp;nm. The resulting RNA product was precipitated with 3.0&nbsp;<span class="smallCaps">m</span> sodium acetate (Bioshop) and resuspended in DNase/RNase-free water. The plasmid map and sequences for IL-12 circRNA were provided in the supplementary information (Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S5</a>, Supporting Information).</p>
                  </section>
               
               <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="adma202400307-sec-0140">
                  
                  <h4 class="article-section__sub-title section4" id="adma202400307-sec-0140-title"> LNP Formulation and Characterization</h4>
                  
                  <p>For high throughput screening, the LNP was formulated by the pipette mixing method as described in previous work.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0044" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0044_R_d171122243e1204" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 44 - Clin. Cancer Res. Gynecol. Oncol. Leuk. Res.">44</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> For the top performing LNP, it was prepared in a microfluidic device using a syringe pump to mix the ethanol and aqueous phases at a volume ratio of 1:3. The ethanol phase was prepared by solubilizing the ionizable lipids ALC-0315, DLin-MC3-DMA, H1L1A1B31, DOPE (Avanti), 1,2-distearoyl-sn-glycero-3-phosphocholine (DSPC), cholesterol (Sigma) and C14-PEG2000 (Avanti). The aqueous phase was prepared in 10 m<span class="smallCaps">m</span> citrate buffer (pH 3.0) with linear mRNA or circRNA preparation. LNP was dialyzed by Slide-A-Lyzer MINI Dialysis Devices (MWCO:20K) (Thermo Fisher) in PBS overnight at 4 °C.</p>
                  
                  <p>For mRNA delivery formulation, the established formulation from the previous study was utilized.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0046" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0046_R_d171122243e1214" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 46 - Int. J. Pharm.">46</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> To optimize circRNA delivery formulations, a definitive screening design experiment was utilized. This aimed to determine the ideal mass ratio of ionizable lipids to circRNA and helper lipids. The preparation of H1L1A1B31 LNPs adhered to the optimized formulation for both IL-12 mRNA and circRNA. Briefly, for mRNA optimized formulation, the molar ratio of ionizable H1L1A1B31 to DOPE, cholesterol, and C14-PEG 2000 was maintained at 35:16:46.5:2.5, respectively. Furthermore, all LNPs were formulated with an ionizable H1L1A1B3 to mRNA mass ratio of 10:1. For circRNA delivery, the formulation ratio was 50:10:38.5:1.5, with the mass ratio of ionizable lipid H1L1A1B3 to circRNA set at 7.5:1.</p>
                  
                  <p>For in vitro and IM administration of LNPs containing H1L1A1B31, the molar ratio of ionizable lipid/DOPE/Chol/C14-PEG 2000 was 35/16/46.5/2.5. LNPs containing H1L1A1B31 for IT administration were formulated with H1L1A1B31/DOTAP/Chol/C14-PEG 2000 at a molar ratio of 30/39/30/1. The LNPs' diameter was assessed using dynamic light scattering (Zetasizer, Malvern). The reported LNP diameters represent the largest intensity mean peak average, which constitutes over 95% of the LNP in the sample. To determine mRNA encapsulation efficiencies, a modified Quanti-iT Ribogreen RNA assay (Invitrogen) was employed, following the previously described procedure.<sup>[</sup><span><sup><a href="#adma202400307-bib-0045" class="bibLink tab-link" id="adma202400307-bib-0045_R_d171122243e1222" data-tab="pane-pcw-references" aria-label="Reference 45 - Nat. Rev. Immunol.">45</a></sup></span><sup>]</sup> ALC-0315 was purchased from Echelon Bioscience. The aqueous phase was prepared with mRNA in 10 mм citrate buffer (pH 4.0, Fisher).</p>
                  </section>
               
               <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="adma202400307-sec-0150">
                  
                  <h4 class="article-section__sub-title section4" id="adma202400307-sec-0150-title"> Cell Culture</h4>
                  
                  <p>LLC1 cells were derived from Lewis lung carcinoma and HKP1 cells were derived from a <i>Kras</i><sup>G12D/+</sup> <i>Trp53</i><sup>−/−</sup> mouse and they were obtained from Dr. Hansen He's laboratory, and the basal media for both cells were Dulbecco's Modified Eagle's Medium (Gibco) and RPMI-1640 (Gibco), respectively, followed by the addition of 10% Fetal bovine serum (Giboco) and penicillin/streptomycin (Gemini) were added. THP1-Dual cells were purchased from InvivoGen and used as directed, containing 2 m<span class="smallCaps">m</span> L-glutamine, 25 m<span class="smallCaps">m</span> HEPES, 10% heat-inactivated fetal bovine serum, 100&nbsp;µg mL<sup>−1</sup> Normocin, Pen-Strep (100 U mL<sup>−1</sup>–100&nbsp;µg mL<sup>−1</sup>) in RPMI 1640 medium according to the instructions. Cells were cultured at 37&nbsp;°C in an incubator containing 5% CO<sub>2</sub> and a certain humidity. Cells were passaged every 2–3 days when confluency reached about 90%. To detect NF-κB and IRF activation in THP1-Dual Cells, the medium was harvested 24 h after transfection and mixed with QUANTI-Lu (Invivogen) and QUANTI-Blue (Invivogen) assay reagents, respectively, according to the instructions to assess Lucia luciferase and SEAP levels. Relative luminescence unit (RLU) in a luminometer and absorbance at 655&nbsp;nm were measured on an Agilent BioTek Cytation 5 microplate reader.</p>
                  </section>
               
               <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="adma202400307-sec-0160">
                  
                  <h4 class="article-section__sub-title section4" id="adma202400307-sec-0160-title"> In Vitro High-Throughput Screening</h4>
                  
                  <p>LNPs for in vitro screening were prepared in a 96-well plate by direct pipette mixing of the ethanol and the aqueous phase as described in the previous work. LNPs/FLuc circRNA complex were added to 96-well plates pre-seeded with LLC1 cells at the dosage level of 100&nbsp;ng FLuc circRNA per 10,000 cells. After overnight incubation, FLuc circRNA transfection potency was measured using the Bright-Glo Luciferase Assay System (Promega) according to the manufacturer's instructions. The relative luminescence unit (RLU) was quantified by the Cytation 1 Cell Imaging Multimode Reader (BioTek).</p>
                  </section>
               
               <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="adma202400307-sec-0170">
                  
                  <h4 class="article-section__sub-title section4" id="adma202400307-sec-0170-title"> Lung Cancer Disease Model Treatment</h4>
                  
                  <p>All animal studies were approved and conducted in compliance with the University Health Network Animal Resources Centre guidelines. Female/male C57BL/6 mice (4–8 weeks) were obtained from the Jackson Laboratory. For the LLC1 tumor model, LLC1 (1 × 10<sup>6</sup>) cells in 100 uL PBS were injected subcutaneously into the left flank of 6–8-week-old C57BL/6 female/male mice weighing 20–25&nbsp;g and randomly assigned to either treatment or control groups. At 1 week when tumors formed, IL-12 circRNA (5&nbsp;µg) was injected into the mouse tumors. For anti-PD-L1 and IL-12 combined immunotherapy, after the single dose of IL-12 circRNA (5&nbsp;µg) on day 9, each mouse was injected intraperitoneally with anti-PD-L1 (200&nbsp;µg, Bio X Cell) for three doses from day 9. Tumor volumes were measured at the indicated times and calculated by 0.5 × length × width.<sup>2</sup> For the orthotopic lung cancer model, HKP1 (1.5 × 10<sup>5</sup>) cells were injected into immune-competent C57BL/6 mice via tail vein. Tumor growth was monitored every 3–7 days by in vivo BLI imaging (Xenogen). Briefly, mice were anesthetized with isoflurane and injected intraperitoneally with <span class="smallCaps">d</span>-luciferin (75&nbsp;mg&nbsp;kg<sup>−1</sup>, Promega). Tumor growth was monitored by BLI using the Xenogen IVIS system coupled with the Living Image Acquisition and Analysis Software (Living Image, Xenogen), and the mice were placed in the supine position after injection of D-fluorescein. IL-12 circRNA LNP was administered via the trachea when in situ lung cancer formation in mice was determined by IVIS.</p>
                  </section>
               
               <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="adma202400307-sec-0180">
                  
                  <h4 class="article-section__sub-title section4" id="adma202400307-sec-0180-title"> BLI In Vitro and In Vivo</h4>
                  
                  <p>For in vitro transfection, 5&nbsp;µg of mRNA or circRNA was prepared in LNP as described above. RNA (200&nbsp;ng) was transfected into 24-well plate (100&nbsp;000 cells well<sup>−1</sup>). For experiments where protein expression was assessed at multiple time points, the medium was completely removed and replaced at each time point. For experiments analyzing SEAP or IRF activity, medium was not changed between transfection and evaluation. For in vivo transfection, IL-12 circRNA (5&nbsp;µg) was formulated as described above and injected intratumorally or intratracheally for the various assays herein. For in vivo transfection, mice were intraperitoneally (IP) injected with 0.2 mL <span class="smallCaps">d</span>-luciferin (10 mg mL<sup>−1</sup> in PBS) after the injection of the circRNA LNPs at 6&nbsp;h. Mice were anesthetized in a ventilated anesthesia chamber with 1.5% isoflurane in oxygen and imaged 10 min after the injection using the IVIS Spectrum CT Imaging System (PerkinElmer). Luminescence was quantified using the Living Image software (PerkinElmer).</p>
                  </section>
               
               <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="adma202400307-sec-0190">
                  
                  <h4 class="article-section__sub-title section4" id="adma202400307-sec-0190-title"> ELISA and Cytokine Analysis</h4>
                  
                  <p>For in vitro assessment of IL-12 circRNA expression, post-transfected LLC1 cells and HKP1 supernatants were collected at the indicated times. For in vivo assessment of IL-12 circRNA expression, mouse blood was collected at the indicated times in different protocols after IL-12 circRNA LNP administration and centrifuged at 3000&nbsp;rpm for 15 min after 30–60 min of standing at room temperature, and the supernatant was collected as mouse serum. Quantification of IL-12 p70 in cell supernatants and plasma was performed using the DuoSet Elisa kit (R&amp;D Systems) according to the manufacturer's instructions. A portion of the serum was used for cytokine assays and Luminex quantification was performed using the Mouse Cytokine Array kit (RayBiotech).</p>
                  </section>
               
               <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="adma202400307-sec-0200">
                  
                  <h4 class="article-section__sub-title section4" id="adma202400307-sec-0200-title"> Flow Cytometry Analysis</h4>
                  
                  <p>To analyze and classify the recruited immune cells, tumor tissues were collected and cut into small pieces, and lung tissues were perfused with PBS and minced. The tissue was digested in a digestion buffer containing RPMI-1640 (Gibco), collagenase/hyaluronidase (Stem Cell), and 1&nbsp;mg mL<sup>−1</sup> deoxyribonuclease I (Worthington) for 20–25&nbsp;min at 37&nbsp;°C. Single-cell suspensions were then made by passing through a 70&nbsp;µm cell strainer. Cells suspended in FACS buffer (PBS + 2% FBS) were blocked with anti-CD16/32 (anti-FcIII/II receptor, clone 93) antibody for 30&nbsp;min. Cells were then incubated with the following fluorescent-conjugated primary antibodies from Biolegend diluted as indicated: CD45 (Clone 30-F11), CD11b (Clone M1/70), Ly6c (Clone HK1.4), Ly6g (Clone 1A8), CD8 (Clone53-6.7), CD4 (Clone GK1.5), CD3 (Clone 17A2), FOXP3 (CloneMF-14), NK1.1 (Clone S17016D). Live/dead dye NIR was obtained from Biolegend (dilution 1:1000). Stained samples were analyzed with a FACS analyzer CytoFlex S (Bekman). All flow cytometry data were analyzed using FlowJo software, and the flow cytometry gating strategy for lymphocyte analysis is shown in Figure <a class="suppLink scrollableLink" href="#support-information-section">S6</a>, Supporting Information.</p>
                  </section>
               
               <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="adma202400307-sec-0210">
                  
                  <h4 class="article-section__sub-title section4" id="adma202400307-sec-0210-title"> Immunohistochemistry, Immunofluorescence, Image, and Processing</h4>
                  
                  <p>Mouse tumor samples from different treatments were incubated in 1% paraformaldehyde at 4&nbsp;°C overnight. Samples were rinsed three times with PBS for 10 min each at room temperature. The samples were placed in PBS containing 30% sucrose and incubated at 4&nbsp;°C overnight, then quickly rinsed with PBS and air-dried. The samples were embedded in tissue cassettes with OCT, placed on a frozen sectioning machine you understand, cut into 8–10&nbsp;µm sections on slides, dried at room temperature for 1 h, then washed with PBS for 5 min three times and dried with paper towels. Incubate with blocking buffer (PBS, 10% FBS, 0.5% Triton-X-100) for 30 min at room temperature. Sections were then covered with the following, fluorescent-conjugated antibodies from Biolegend and incubated overnight: CD8 (Clone 53-6.7), IFN-γ (Clone XMG1.2), CD31 (Clone MEC13.3), CD45 (Clone 30-F11). Sections were washed three times with PBS for 5&nbsp;min, air-dried, and incubated for 20&nbsp;min covered with the nuclear dye DAPI (Thermo Fisher). Stained slides were imaged using a Zeiss LCSM 700 confocal and images were processed using Zen software. H&amp;E staining and histology were processed and analyzed by the UHN Pathology Laboratory and the Advanced Optical Microscopy Facility.</p>
                  </section>
               
               <section class="article-section__sub-content" id="adma202400307-sec-0220">
                  
                  <h4 class="article-section__sub-title section4" id="adma202400307-sec-0220-title"> Statistics</h4>
                  
                  <p>All analyses were analyzed using GraphPad Prism statistical software (GraphPad Software Inc.) A two-tailed Student's <i>t</i>-test or a one-way analysis of variance (ANOVA) was performed when comparing two groups or more than two groups, respectively. Data are expressed as means ± s.d. Difference was significant if <i>p</i> &lt; 0.05. * = <i>p</i>-value &lt; 0.05, ** = <i>p</i>-value &lt; 0.01, *** = <i>p</i>-value &lt; 0.005.</p>
                  </section>
               </section>
            </section>
         </section>
